Every method in this cluster surfaces an explanation from that structure. The question this survey poses to them is the one it poses elsewhere: does any verify that a surfaced statistic is causally load-bearing for the move actually chosen, or does it stop at describing the tree?

The earliest systematic framing comes from Baier and Kaisers [55], who assemble a toolset of building blocks for explaining MCTS decisions on Connect Four and Breakthrough. Every explanation derives from two post-search operations: tree simplification, which selects the principal variation and its siblings, and subtree summarization, which abstracts a subtree into the probabilities of pre-specified, domain-meaningful scenarios estimated from the proportion of simulations reaching them. The system also offers collaborative commands (“think about,” “expect response,” “search together”) that re-run the search under user-supplied focus and report whether the decision changed, framing explanation as dialogue rather than one-way output. It is a position paper with no user study or metric, and its explanations remain correlational summaries of simulation proportions; nothing tests whether a summarized scenario is load-bearing. The collaborative re-search command is nonetheless the conceptual seed of a search-side intervention, since it perturbs the search and re-reads the outcome without yet making that perturbation a measured causal test.

Kowalski et al. [34] extend the descriptive approach while staying deliberately knowledge-free. Rather than encode game rules, they repurpose the internal statistics of standard MCTS enhancements as explanation material: MAST and its n-gram extension NST for context-free move values, GRAVE/AMAF for cross-phase estimates, Score-Bounded MCTS for pessimistic and optimistic terminal bounds, and proof-number search for the proof workload on a subtree. Built atop the Ludii general game-playing system, the proof-of-concept surfaces these quantities as mixed numeric-and-narrative explanations across six example positions, arguing they are richer than visit counts alone. The work is explicitly preliminary, with no user study, no quantitative explanation-quality metric, and no test that a surfaced statistic drives the chosen move. Its knowledge-free stance also holds explanations at the level of opaque counters and n-grams rather than human-meaningful tactics such as pins or hanging pieces, which are precisely the factors a move-level benchmark must grade.

A second strand pursues rigor by grounding explanations in formal structure. An et al. [36] translate user requirements into Computation Tree Logic (CTL) specifications via natural-language templates, model-check the MCTS tree against them, and render the verification results back into readable text. A study of 82 participants found the resulting explanations preferred over baselines. Their follow-up [37] couples the logic backbone to LLMs: an LLM classifies a query into one of 26 post-hoc types, few-shot prompting compiles it into typed logic statements, CTL model-checking and scorer functions extract evidence from the tree, and a QA model synthesizes an answer, sharply raising automated factual-consistency scores (FactCC, BERTScore) over raw GPT and Llama baselines on a paratransit-planning task. Qian et al. [38] take a different structural route, integrating shallow minimax into MCTS rollouts and then applying process mining to event logs of agent behavior, extracting process models that visualize the agents’ decision strategies in 3v3 checkers.

These accounts source their evidence more rigorously than the descriptive readouts, yet their evaluation targets expose the same gap. An et al. measure user preference [36] and automated factual consistency against tree evidence [37]. Qian et al. visualize aggregate behavioral patterns [38]. Preference, factual consistency, and process visualization each certify that an explanation is grounded in the search, not that the cited factor is causally load-bearing for the decision the search produced.

Lu et al. [35] react against the brittleness of template- and logic-based pipelines with a template-free, LLM-driven explainer. The pipeline maps a natural-language question to a structured intent, checks whether the current tree holds enough evidence to answer it, and, when it does not, triggers targeted subtree expansion before generating an answer grounded in tree statistics. It handles node-, path-, and behavior-level questions, including contrastive and counterfactual queries. Evaluated only on FrozenLake with 21 hand-authored question-tree pairs and no baselines, its “grounding” is checked by a keyword heuristic the authors themselves call preliminary, a surface proxy rather than a causal test of which statistics drove the decision. The mechanism is nonetheless the most suggestive in the cluster: expanding a specific subtree to answer a specific question is, structurally, an intervention on the search itself.

Read together, this literature establishes the search tree as a legitimate explanation surface and builds up a vocabulary for operating on it: simplifying it [55], mining enhancement statistics from it [34], model-checking it [36], [37], abstracting it into process models [38], and expanding targeted subtrees of it [35]. What none supplies is a causal test of load-bearingness. Every method reads or grounds an explanation in the tree, and the two cases that come closest to perturbing the search, Baier and Kaisers’ collaborative re-search [55] and Lu et al.’s targeted expansion [35], stop short of treating a decision delta as the explanation’s warrant. This is the surface a later extension of factor-level causal grading could occupy. Intervening inside a search subtree, by pruning or forcing a branch and re-reading the agent’s choice, would be complementary to the board- and rule-level factor interventions that are this survey’s subject, not a competitor to them: one grades the reasons a position affords a strong oracle, the other would grade the reasons the search itself commits to. The MCTS-explanation literature is therefore best read here as a deferred intervention surface, broad in its grounding machinery and uniform in stopping one step short of the verified causal test.

10. Game-AI Explanation Beyond Chess

The explanation problem this survey traces is not native to chess. Card games pose the same two-part demand, a system that both acts well and says why, under the harder condition of hidden state, and two recent lines of work show each half emerging there on its own. Surveying them fixes the outer boundary of the survey’s scope and tests whether its central move survives a change of game.

Tao et al. [57] build an LLM commentary generator for Guandan, a four-player imperfect-information card game. The system couples reinforcement-learning scenario generation with three language modules: a state guide that grounds narration in the current position, a Theory-of-Mind analyzer that reasons over opponents’ concealed hands, and a style-retrieval component that imitates professional commentators. On open-source backbones it reportedly surpasses GPT-4 across several commentary-quality metrics. The output is exactly the kind of factor-citing narration the intervention lens is built to grade, naming the strategic reason a play is strong. Its evaluation stops at stylistic and fluency proxies, judging whether the commentary sounds expert rather than whether the cited factor actually drives the optimal play. The Theory-of-Mind reasoning is asserted rather than tested against the true hidden state, and the imperfect-information setting denies the system any clean oracle to intervene against. This is the stop-point Movement III met in every chess commentator: a rich explanation source with no test of causal load-bearing.

Wang et al. [58] ask the complementary question, whether LLMs can master complex card games as decisively as AlphaZero mastered chess and Go. They supervised-fine-tune models on strong-AI gameplay across eight card games and report three findings: fine-tuned LLMs approach specialized game-AI strength; a single model can learn several games at once, more readily when the games share rules; and deep game mastery erodes general instruction-following, a catastrophic-forgetting trade-off partly recovered by mixing general instruction data into training. The study concerns competence, not justification, and carries no move-explanation channel and no faithfulness or causal-intervention machinery. It matters here as the competence half without the explanation half: strong play that no one has yet asked to account for itself.

Read together, the two papers suggest—but, resting on only two works neither of which runs the intervention test, do not establish—that the survey’s central move could generalize. The intervention-grounded lens, neutralize a cited factor and re-measure the decision, asks nothing chess-specific in principle, and it would apply to any decision a system can be made to take again under a controlled change. What chess supplies, and what Guandan does not, is the machinery that makes the re-measurement clean: rule-decidable factors and a strong, queryable oracle over fully observable state. Imperfect information removes the oracle and blurs the counterfactual, so card games can host the explanation problem but not yet its verifiable evaluation. On this thin two-paper base the generalization is a conjecture, not a demonstrated result; chess is therefore best read as a training ground for the method, not its endpoint. Once the recipe is validated where decision-level ground truth is computable, the open task is to carry it toward the harder, partially observable settings where these systems will eventually be asked to explain themselves.

11. Synthesis: where xai-chess fits

The three movements of this survey each deposited a premise. Movement I established that the faithfulness-evaluation literature converged on a mature recipe yet remains starved of verified labels; Movement II established that chess networks carry decidable, rule-grounded causal structure and admit strong oracles; Movement III established that real systems emit move-explanations and that the nearest prior art grades them only at the board level. This chapter spends those premises. It states the project’s five-part thesis against the surveyed evidence and, as the only chapter licensed to do so, reports xai-chess’s own results. The compressed claim is that the method is settled, the binding constraint is ground truth, two ground-truth targets must be kept apart, and chess is the substrate that supplies the verifiable one.

11.1 The method is settled; the bottleneck is ground truth

The chain-of-thought community has already converged, by independent reinvention, on the evaluation recipe xai-chess borrows: neutralize a cited factor with a matched baseline and test statistically whether the decision moves. The recipe instances are Lanham [29], RFEval [16], and ICE [17], with Turpin’s planted-bias input interventions [28] as the input-side proto-instance and Jacovi’s definition of the target [30] behind them all. ICE [17] supplies the full statistical stack, a win-rate primary metric with Cohen’s d, bootstrap confidence intervals, and Benjamini-Hochberg FDR control, reported comparatively across intervention operators. RFEval [16] arrives at counterfactual reasoning interventions and the conclusion that accuracy is no proxy for faithfulness. A second tier of meta-evaluators sits one level up, grading the metrics themselves rather than instantiating the recipe: Causal Diagnosticity [15] scores metrics on edit-based counterfactual pairs, BonaFide [14] meta-evaluates a whole suite of perturbation metrics against ground truth, and BUMP [44] does the same over unfaithful minimal pairs. When several teams redundantly rediscover one pattern, that pattern is shared field property, not a contribution to be claimed. Here the borrowing must be stated precisely, because it sharpens rather than blurs the project’s own thesis. xai-chess adopts the statistical apparatus wholesale—matched baseline, randomization test, win rate, Cohen’s d, bootstrap CI, BH-FDR, multi-operator reporting—but redirects it to a different estimand. The recipe instances above are target-1 instruments: they intervene on the explainer’s own reasoning or inputs and read the explainer’s own decision, estimating self-faithfulness on the system under test. xai-chess instead intervenes on the world—the board or the rules—and reads a separate oracle’s decision, estimating decision-level causal load-bearing (the second target). The explainer whose claim is graded and the oracle intervened upon are different systems, so xai-chess grades reason-correctness against an oracle, not faithfulness to any explainer. Granting the shared apparatus is what isolates the real contribution.

That mature recipe is universally short of verified labels. BonaFide [14] builds a costly labeling pipeline and still finds its best chain-of-thought-level metric reaches only 0.70 AUROC, with most metrics near chance. Causal Diagnosticity [15] manufactures edit-based labels that go unverified, and its own analysis reports that models often ignore the edit, forcing a fall-back to synthetic explanations. ICE [17] substitutes randomized matched baselines precisely because per-item labels do not exist. The 0.70 ceiling under hand-curated labels shows the limiting factor is the labels themselves, not the metric arithmetic. Throughout this synthesis the 0.70 figure serves as a reference bar to clear, not as evidence of a failed field.

11.2 Two ground-truth targets must not be conflated

“Faithfulness ground truth” silently names two different things, and the survey’s central conceptual move is to separate them. The first is explainer-internal faithfulness: whether an explanation reflects the explainer’s own computation. Jacovi [30] fixes this as the field’s stated target and distinguishes it from mere plausibility. The second is decision-level causal load-bearing: whether the cited factor is actually doing causal work for a competent decision. Sandmann [5] proves the two come apart, showing that a chess network’s internal solutions, including found mates, are systematically overridden by later layers before output, so internal computation and emitted behavior can legitimately diverge. McGrath [1] then shows that the chess-native instrument, concept probing, is explicitly correlational and names the pin / can_capture_queen confound: a concept can be decodable without being causally used. Probing therefore does not deliver the first target, and only intervention can certify the second.

This disambiguation forces a lexical discipline that doubles as the answer to the strongest objection against the project, that measuring something other than the explainer’s internals makes the word “faithfulness” a category error. The project concedes the word. It does not claim to read the graded commentator’s unobservable internals; it claims only that each cited factor is causally load-bearing for a strong oracle’s decision, a grounding and correctness property complementary to internal faithfulness rather than a replacement for it. Because the two targets are genuinely distinct, naming the measured property “faithfulness” would itself be the error. Declining the term is the rigorous choice, and the project lives entirely on the decision-level side of the distinction.

11.3 Chess as the substrate

Chess co-locates three properties that text cannot supply together, which is what lets the intervention recipe finally run against a verifiable label. Factor presence is decidable by the rules, so detection via python-chess is exact by construction. Decision quality is read from a strong oracle. The empirical results reported in §11.5 use Stockfish 18 at a fixed 400k-node, single-thread budget, which is deterministic, as the oracle that supplies the per-position valuation the win-probability and log-odds scales are read from. A searchless action-value head such as Ruoss’s amortized planner [7] is an attractive candidate alternative oracle—it yields a calibrated, search-free per-move win-probability that avoids any centipawn-to-probability conversion—but it is discussed here from the literature and did not produce the numbers below. The causal structure each factor implicates is independently attested as real and testable inside chess networks (Ch. 6; e.g., [4], [6]). McGrath’s pin / can_capture_queen confound [1] is exactly the correlational ambiguity that a causal intervention dissolves, and chess hands the analyst the precise factor to neutralize. SHAP-for-chess [19] further demonstrates that intervening on the board and reading an evaluation delta is feasible in this domain. The ground truth is the causal fact, whether suspending the pin changes the best continuation under the rules, made legible by the oracle rather than reducible to the oracle’s preference. No other surveyed substrate brings decidable detection, a clean win-probability oracle, and attested causal structure into one place, which is why chess is the cleanest venue for the decision-level target, not merely one venue among many.

11.4 Novelty, scoped honestly

The contribution, stated narrowly, is not non-text-ness as such—prior causal interventions already operate on non-text substrates—but a confound-free rule-level intervention surface together with a measurable agreement between intervention surfaces, both graded against a separate oracle with win-probability deltas as treatment effects. The claim is established by differencing against the nearest neighbors, which must be named as competing “firsts” rather than mere templates. Atrey [32] already performs state-level, environment-grounded counterfactual interventions on a non-text RL substrate; what it lacks is a separate decision oracle, the statistical intervention-consistency layer, and the move-factor target, so it is the closest non-text precedent the scoped claim must be differenced from rather than a counterexample to it. SHAP-for-chess [19] has the board-level remove-a-piece move but attributes static board occupancy rather than move-explanation factors, evaluates qualitatively over seven illustrative positions, and offers no confound-free surface, no agreement test, and no ground-truth framing. A third neighbor must now be named, because it is the only prior work that grades against a causal ground truth in chess. Pálsson and Björnsson retrain the Stockfish NNUE with a concept’s training positions removed and read the concept’s importance off a nine-thousand-game Elo gauntlet against the intact network [66]. That is a genuine causal ground truth, and it establishes the design pattern of grading an interpretability claim against a measured strength effect rather than against a human label. It differs on the two axes that matter here. Its intervention is on the training data, so the object compared is a differently-trained network rather than the same engine under a changed rule, which introduces exactly the network-identity confound the rule-level surface is built to avoid; and its target is a probing method’s importance ranking, not a factor cited by an explanation for a particular move, so it never reaches decision-level load-bearing. The scoped claim must therefore be differenced from three neighbors, not two, and the honest summary is that Pálsson and Björnsson got to causal ground truth in chess first, by a different route and for a different target. Read against these three, the precise and defensible claim is the conjunction—oracle-backed AND statistical intervention-consistency AND a confound-free rule-level surface AND measurable multi-surface agreement on a move-factor target—not a bare “first on a non-text substrate.” ICE [17] supplies the precedent that operator-comparative reporting is a valued output rather than a defect. Causal Diagnosticity [15] supplies the thing to improve on: grade behavior against an oracle instead of against an unverified edit. A win-probability oracle is what makes “treatment effect” well defined in the first place—Stockfish’s evaluation in the reported results (§11.3), with a searchless value head such as Ruoss’s [7] as a candidate alternative.

The recurring objection is that this is “just SHAP-for-chess with extra steps,” and the rebuttal is to show the extra step changes the verdict. The board-level surface relocates a piece, which carries a confound: moving the pinned-to king off the ray also perturbs king-safety, so it moves more than the one property under test. The rule-level surface, a roughly twenty-line movegen patch to Stockfish exposed as an opt-in MaskPinner UCI option (the patched build stockfish-xai-rulelevel; with the option off it is byte-identical to stock), instead suspends the pin in the rules with no piece moved, letting the pinned piece move while denying the pinning slider any check or capture along the ray. A stock engine cannot reproduce this without literally exposing the king, which would invert the signal. “Confound-free” must be qualified honestly, however: the rule-level surface removes the board-relocation confound but trades it for a possible engine-modification confound, since the patched search now explores positions illegal under standard rules and could in principle perturb the evaluation landscape in ways unrelated to the pin. Two pieces of evidence suggest the trade is benign—the MaskPinner default leaves the build byte-identical to stock, so non-pin behavior is unchanged whenever the option is off, and the rule-level surface agrees with the board-level surface on every golden pin case (§11.5)—but a dedicated null control (patched-vs-stock evaluation on pin-free positions) is left to future work, so the defensible claim is “free of the board-relocation confound,” not free of all confounds. The relocation-confound-free surface and the agreement test are the differentiators, and the measured gap between surfaces, reported next, is what proves the added step is decisive rather than decorative.

11.5 The project’s empirical evidence

What follows is the survey’s one embedded original contribution, reported as such: these are the authors’ own results, produced by the open xai-chess repository and pending external validation, not third-party evidence surveyed from the literature. The reproducibility basics are stated up front. The oracle is Stockfish 18 at a fixed 400k-node, single-thread budget, which is deterministic; the rule-level surface uses the movegen-patched build stockfish-xai-rulelevel (opt-in MaskPinner, default byte-identical to stock). The corpus is the Lichess puzzle database (CC0), N = 164 (83 pin-themed “faithful” / 81 incidental-pin “red herring”), with labels assigned by Lichess tactic themes and therefore independent of the metric under test. The pipeline is open and reproducible from the repository: lichess_ingest.py builds the JSONL corpus, corpus_eval.py computes AUROC with bootstrap confidence intervals, and baselines.py supplies the random and saliency baselines.

The apparatus is a factor-typed DSL in which each factor type supplies detect, to_nl, and remove. Two types are implemented: pin, which admits both surfaces, and hanging, which is board-level only because nothing “hanging” is a rule of chess to suspend. Outcomes are read on three scales. Win-probability change, eff_wp, is bounded and interpretable but saturates near won or lost positions; log-odds change, eff_logit, de-saturates the tails and is the robust default; centipawn change, eff_cp, never saturates but is noisy at the extremes. On six golden cases (three pin, three hanging), load-bearing citations and red herrings separate cleanly at eff_wp ≥ 0.10, and the two surfaces agree on every pin case. A saturated but genuinely load-bearing position exposes the eff_wp ceiling, registering a false “red herring” at +0.078 that eff_cp ≈ +197cp and eff_logit ≈ +0.73 recover, and the hanging quarantine path fires correctly when the cited piece is in fact defended.

The Lichess meta-evaluation uses CC0 puzzles with labels independent of the metric, taking pin-themed puzzles as the load-bearing (positive) class and incidental-pin non-pin puzzles as red herrings, with N = 164 (83 positive, 81 red herring) scored over 400k Stockfish-18 oracle nodes (§11.3), all scorers run on the same records.

Scorer AUROC 95% CI
Random 0.57 [0.49, 0.66]
Saliency (move targets pinned material) 0.61 [0.54, 0.68]
Board-level eff_wp 0.69 [0.60, 0.77]
Rule-level eff_wp 0.82 [0.75, 0.88]

The rule-level interval lies entirely above the 0.70 BonaFide reference bar [14], the only scorer that clears it. Paired bootstraps confirm the ordering is not noise: rule minus saliency is +0.21 [+0.12, +0.30] and rule minus board is +0.13 [+0.04, +0.23], both excluding zero. The headline follows directly: which intervention surface the analyst uses decides whether the metric works at all, a contrast visible only because the causal effect is computable, which is exactly the affordance chess supplies.

Two hazards must be named where the evidence is reported. The first is circularity: a metric is worthless as its own label. A forced-mate “verified-label” probe is genuinely circular for the rule-level metric, because the label and the metric share the same counterfactual of suspending the pin, so that probe is retired and reported only as a cautionary artifact. The Lichess evaluation avoids the trap by sourcing its label exogenously from the corpus’s own theme tag, computed by no intervention. The second is thinness: a single factor, a single oracle, and a noisy proxy tag. This is conceded as scope. The defensible result is the contrast, not the absolute number, and the rule ≫ board ≫ saliency ordering with paired intervals excluding zero is robust to label noise even where the absolute AUROC is measured against a proxy.

11.6 Surface disagreement is informative, not a bug

A natural worry is that an oracle will score a perfectly sound human explanation as non-load-bearing on a position that is already won. The reframe is that this is a measurement-scale effect, not a verdict on the human. On a won position the win-probability is saturated, so neutralizing even a genuinely cited factor barely moves eff_wp, which is precisely the saturated load-bearing golden case, and the de-saturating scales eff_cp and eff_logit recover the true signal. The broader point is that metric-human divergence is expected. ICE [17] finds operator gaps up to 44 percentage points and essentially zero correlation between faithfulness and human plausibility (|r| < 0.04), and Sandmann [5] supplies the mechanism, since internal solutions found and then discarded mean activation-level and behavioral interventions can legitimately disagree. Disagreement across surfaces, scales, or against human judgment is a finding the substrate makes legible, and surfacing it is part of the contribution.

12. Limitations of the surveyed field and open problems

The project’s own boundaries are worth stating plainly, because they recur across the field rather than being peculiar to it. The Lichess pin tag is an independent but noisy proxy: the rule-versus-baseline contrasts are robust to that noise, yet the absolute AUROC is measured against a proxy and not against verified ground truth. The proxy’s failure modes are now specific enough to state rather than gesture at, because the tagger is open. Themes are produced by cook.py in the lichess-puzzler repository, a flat cascade of Python predicates over python-chess board queries. Three properties follow. The detectors are puzzle-level, firing if any move in the solution line exhibits the motif, so a tag is not an assertion about the move under test. They return a boolean and discard the witness, so fork counts more than one attacked piece without recording which, and a tag therefore cannot be checked against the squares an explanation cites. And the natural-language theme definitions shipped in puzzleTheme.xml are maintained separately from the predicates with no formal link between them, so the prose meaning and the computed meaning can drift without anything detecting it. Of the roughly seventy themes, only zugzwang is engine-grounded, and it alone is a genuine counterfactual: push a null move, re-analyze, and fire on the win-probability gap. For pin these properties are no longer inferred from reading the tagger but measured against it. The two predicates behind the tag, pin_prevents_attack and pin_prevents_escape, have been re-expressed per position and checked against the vendored original on all 6,100,960 puzzles in the export with zero disagreements, and the tag they compute reproduces the exported pin theme on 99.76% of the probe set. Two consequences follow that a reading of the prose definition would not have surfaced. The tag is carried almost entirely by one branch: pin_prevents_attack fires on 26.06% of the probe set against pin_prevents_escape’s 3.95%. And neither predicate ever inspects the position the solver faces, since both loop over mainline[1::2], the boards after each of the solver’s moves; the tag is a property of positions one ply past the one under test. The tag vocabulary is therefore best read as a rule-derived weak label, and the honest reading of the absolute AUROC is that it measures agreement with a hand-written rule cascade, not with verified ground truth. Only random and saliency are ported as same-task baselines, because BonaFide’s other six metrics [14] are chain-of-thought-specific with no chess analogue, which makes the 0.70 figure a cross-domain reference bar rather than a head-to-head competitor. The demonstration rests on one factor type and one oracle. The forced-mate “verified-label” probe is circular for the rule-level metric, since label and metric share the counterfactual, and has been retired; catching and removing a circular item is a sign of rigor, not a wound.

These boundaries mirror weaknesses that run through the surveyed corpus. Labels are proxies almost everywhere, whether unverified edits [15], plausibility-bound simulatability scores, or noisy human tags, and the field rarely confirms that the entity supposedly under test was the entity the model actually used. Interpretability repeatedly stops at correlation, beginning with the canonical chess probing work [1] and its successors, which establish decodability without establishing causal use, so a high probe accuracy proves a representation is present, never that it is load-bearing. One chess work does not stop there, and it is the closest existing precedent to this survey’s design, so it should be engaged rather than left for a reader to find. Pálsson and Björnsson construct a causal ground truth for concept importance by retraining the Stockfish NNUE with a concept’s positions skipped from the training stream, then playing the ablated network against the unmodified one over nine thousand games at three search depths and reading the strength difference as Elo [66]. Probing methods are then scored against that Elo drop, and the result is a direct rebuke to probe accuracy as an importance measure: raw probe accuracy correlates at 0.69 to 0.73, while the differential between probes on the intact and concept-removed networks reaches 0.83 to 0.92. Two differences bound how far it reaches. It ablates training data rather than rules, so the concept is removed from what the network learned rather than from what the position affords, and the retrained network is a different network, which is a confound the rule-level surface avoids by leaving the engine untouched. And it grades interpretability methods, not stated reasons, so it never asks whether a factor an explanation cites is load-bearing for a particular move. The gauntlet design is nonetheless directly reusable, and the raw-versus-differential gap is independent evidence for the survey’s central claim that decodability and causal use come apart. Mechanistic surfaces are fragile under net-binding: the strongest internal causal results are pinned to exact network checkpoints (Leela in [4], BT4 in [6], and a separate action-value transformer in [7]), and the relevant features cannot always be ablated inside the host engine, so a mechanistic intervention is only as portable as the one checkpoint it was validated on. Single deterministic verdicts without a randomization layer also persist, as in RFEval’s single indicator [16], leaving the field without a principled null against which “no effect” can be read.

The agenda these gaps imply is concrete. First, surface-agreement deserves promotion from an internal robustness check to a primary reported result. ICE [17] already treats operator gaps of up to 44 percentage points as informative rather than as noise to be averaged away, and Sandmann [5] supplies the mechanism for why surfaces should be expected to diverge, since computation that is found and then discarded means board-level, rule-level, and any future activation-level intervention need not agree. A benchmark that reports cross-surface agreement as a first-class quantity, with its own confidence intervals, would convert a sanity check into evidence about where and why explanations and oracles part company. Second, a third, mechanistic intervention surface, internal feature ablation in the style of the look-ahead and transcoder work, could join the board-level and rule-level surfaces, but only if it is bound to one exact network hash, since the net-binding fragility above makes any mechanistic claim checkpoint-relative. Third, the factor DSL is presently hand-authored, with pin and hanging written by hand; extracting cited factors automatically from free-text commentary (Ch. 8; e.g., [12]) is open and is what would let the benchmark grade real explanation sources at scale rather than curated cases. Three partial answers already exist and none has been connected to a causal test. Bizjak and Guid define a term-level motif vocabulary that separates static position terms from dynamic terms describing the solution line, and report that the dynamic terms carry most of the retrieval signal — top-1 accuracy 0.418 for dynamic alone against 0.252 for static alone, on 400 expert-paired puzzles [71]. That asymmetry is direct evidence for this survey’s premise that a move’s reason is not recoverable from the position alone, and their token set is the nearest thing in the literature to a factor vocabulary a benchmark could consume. Caïssa’s Prolog layer supplies the complementary piece, since its predicates return the witness pieces for a fork or a pin rather than a boolean, which is exactly the structure a factor-level intervention needs and exactly what the Lichess tags discard [54]. And chess has carried a standardized, language-independent annotation vocabulary since 1994 in the PGN Numeric Annotation Glyphs [86], which no explanation system in this survey emits, though doing so would make outputs gradeable against a fixed vocabulary and comparable across systems. A factor extractor built from a motif vocabulary, emitting witnesses, in a standard glyph set, is a concrete and unclaimed piece of work. Fourth, evaluation should be stratified by position difficulty, since the saturation behavior reported above shows that effect sizes are not comparable across won, drawn, and sharp positions; the human-difficulty calibrators surveyed in Ch. 7 are the natural instrument for binning positions before scoring. Finally, whether the methodology transfers beyond chess is at present a conjecture, not a result: it rests on only two cross-domain works ([57], [58]), neither of which actually runs the intervention test. The methodology should transfer wherever a domain offers both decidable factors and a strong oracle, so porting the rule-versus-board surface-agreement test beyond chess, toward the imperfect-information card-game settings at the survey’s outer boundary, is the proper—and still unrun—test of whether the contribution is chess-specific or a general template for grading the reasons behind a decision.

13. Conclusion

The faithfulness-evaluation field has converged on a mature method and is now limited by something the method cannot provide. Neutralize a cited factor with a matched baseline and test statistically whether the decision moves: ICE [17], RFEval [16], Causal Diagnosticity [15], and BonaFide [14] all instantiate this recipe, which is therefore shared property rather than any single project’s novelty. What every instantiation lacks is verified ground truth, and BonaFide’s 0.70 ceiling under costly hand labels [14] marks the bottleneck precisely. The same shortage shows up at both ends of the spectrum, in the unverified edit labels of Causal Diagnosticity [15] and in the randomized baselines ICE [17] adopts because per-item labels do not exist, which is why the constraint is now the substrate rather than the statistics.

The survey’s organizing distinction is that two ground-truth targets have been conflated. Explainer-internal faithfulness, whether an explanation reflects the explainer’s own computation [30], is genuinely separable from decision-level causal load-bearing, whether a cited factor does causal work, and Sandmann’s evidence that internal solutions are overridden before output [5] shows the two can come apart. Chess supplies the second target verifiably, by combining rule-decidable factor detection, a strong win-probability oracle (Stockfish for the reported results, with a searchless value head [7] as a candidate alternative), and attested rule-grounded causal structure, a combination no other surveyed substrate offers.

On that substrate, xai-chess contributes a confound-free rule-level intervention surface and a measurable agreement between intervention surfaces, graded against a separate oracle with win-probability deltas as treatment effects. The scoped novelty is that conjunction, differenced from the nearest non-text precedent, Atrey’s state-level RL counterfactuals [32], which lack a separate oracle and the statistical layer, and from SHAP-for-chess [19], which has only the board-level surface and none of the framing—not a bare claim of being first on a non-text substrate. The decisive lesson, reported here as the authors’ own result pending external validation rather than as surveyed third-party evidence, is that intervention-surface choice determines whether the metric works, with the confound-free rule-level surface clearing the reference bar that board-level and saliency scorers do not. That result is not a chess curiosity; it is a warning that any intervention-based evaluation can pass or fail on the operator alone, which is invisible in domains where no computable causal effect lets the surfaces be compared.

The contribution is thus a validated substrate and a grounding property that complements, and does not replace, explainer-internal faithfulness. The scope is honest about its limits, a single factor, a single oracle, and a noisy proxy tag, yet the contrast it demonstrates is robust where the absolute numbers are not. For a field that has the method but not the labels, chess offers a setting in which the recipe can finally be tested against ground truth it cannot otherwise obtain, and in which the gap between a plausible reason and a load-bearing one becomes a quantity rather than a hope.

AI-generated

Move-level chess XAI — what exists

A landscape survey of systems that explain a specific chess move, in natural language or in a formal representation, and of the datasets behind them.

Compiled 2026-08-01 from six parallel research passes: natural-language commentary, LLM-era and commercial systems, concept and mechanistic interpretability, symbolic and DSL explanation, datasets, and evaluation.

This is a companion to paper/survey.md, not a replacement. That document argues a thesis about causal-intervention evaluation. This one inventories what exists.

Every claim traces to a fetched source. Anything that could not be confirmed that way is marked UNVERIFIED inline and must not be cited on this document’s authority.

1. Bottom line

Four systems in fifty years explain a specific move symbolically: PARADISE (1980), Automated Chess Tutor (2006), CCC (2025), Caïssa AI (2025).

Everything else classifies whole puzzles, analyses the model globally, or attributes to squares without naming a motif.

No formal grammar of chess motifs exists. Three artifacts come close, and none denotes “the reason this move is good”: CQL queries positions, Bizjak & Guid tokenise motifs for retrieval, cook.py is imperative Python booleans.

Nobody measures whether an explanation is true. Every generation system is scored on fluency, human preference, LLM-judge rating, or downstream move accuracy. None checks whether an asserted tactical claim holds on the board.

No benchmark measures causal faithfulness of chess move explanations. The one chess explanation benchmark that exists grades agreement with a human expert vote — the plausibility axis.

Causal work and concept work are disjoint populations. Strong causal machinery explains via squares and future moves. Human concept vocabularies come with no intervention. One 2026 preprint spans both, and it is unreviewed.

2. Definitions

Three notions are routinely conflated in this literature. The rest of this document depends on keeping them apart.

Faithfulness — the explanation reflects the system’s actual decision process. Established by intervention or by ground truth, never by asking a human.

Plausibility — the explanation convinces a human, or matches a human-written reference. This is what almost every chess metric measures.

Simulatability — a human can predict the agent’s behaviour given the explanation. A third axis, and not a proxy for either of the others.

Jacovi & Goldberg (ACL 2020) supply the canonical statement and three guidelines that indict most of the work below: faithfulness evaluation must not use human judgement of interpretation quality, human-provided gold labels, or user performance.

Chen et al. (ICML 2024) settle the empirical question. Counterfactual-simulation precision correlates with human plausibility ratings at r = +0.012, against inter-annotator plausibility agreement of +0.388. The axes are orthogonal.

3. The grounding problem

Before asking whether an explanation is true, you must know what it is about. Chess work has not addressed this. Shogi work solved it in 2015.

Kameko, Mori & Tsuruoka, IEEE CIG 2015, pp. 177–184. Their insight: a commentary sentence is usually not about the current position. It refers to a hypothetical continuation or a past line.

So the comment’s move expressions must first be grounded to a node in the game tree. They enumerate candidate trees over the current position plus the previous three moves, inserting Pass for lines that are illegal but commented.

Selection uses engine evaluations, Ev_T(t) = Σ_m Ev_M(m) − max_m Ev_M(m) + B_P + B_B. The heuristic: experts comment on bad moves by showing the right move, so the correct tree is the one not containing a sequence of bad moves.

Generation is a 3-layer perceptron predicting a characteristic-word vector from the engine’s evaluation features, then a log-linear language model conditioned on position plus predicted words.

Results: at least one candidate tree for 44,166 of 54,084 positions (81.2%); of 100 hand-checked, the correct tree for 79. There is no BLEU and no human study — grounding accuracy and qualitative figures only.

Their own error analysis is the load-bearing part. About 20% of move-expression comments produce no candidates and about 20% select the wrong tree, and both “directly affect the performance of the trained model.”

Every chess system assumes the comment refers to the current position, and several then blame that assumption for hallucination. This sits upstream of faithfulness: you cannot check a claim against a position until you know which position.

Predecessor: Kaneko, IPSJ Journal 53(11):2525–2532, 2012 (Japanese), template-based over search output. Attested only as a reference in the Kameko PDF, so bibliography-grade. Kameko’s critique — template variety “is much smaller than that of human experts.”

Go commentary generation: nothing found. The nearest work (Tomlin, He & Klein, ACL 2022, arXiv:2204.07531) probes AlphaGo Zero for domain terms such as ko and atari. That is interpretation, not generation.

4. Systems that explain a specific move

4a. Natural language

System Yr Engine in loop? Symbolic intermediate Eval of the explanation
Jhamtani et al., GameKnot commentary 2018 No (board features) Hand-coded board/attack features BLEU + human validity
Butner, ChessCoach 2021 Yes (own AZ-style) No Author: “often wrong”
Lee, Wu, Dinan & Lewis 2022 Yes (Leela) 5 discrete control tags Human A/B, perplexity
Kim et al., CCC 2025 Yes (Lc0 probes + SF8) 20 scored concepts GCC-Eval + 5 raters
Tang et al., C1 2026 Yes (SF depth-24 PV) PV trace, not logic None — move accuracy only
Cui, Ling & Ng 2026 Yes (SF16 strategy tree) Strategy tree (JSON) Downstream puzzle-playing utility
DecodeChess (commercial) 2019–23 Yes (SF search verifies) Verified concept predicates None published
ChessBase Fritz “Full analysis” 1996– Yes Tactical pattern rules 3× ICCA Herschberg Award

The published field is smaller than it looks. A DBLP sweep plus enumeration of all 46 papers citing Jhamtani puts the complete list of published game-commentary generators in 1990–2022 at six.

Those six: Liao & Chang 1990 (Othello), Sadikov et al. 2006, Guid et al. 2008, Jhamtani et al. 2018, Zang et al. 2019, Lee et al. 2022. The commercial systems in §4b predate all of them and published nothing.

Worth stating if you build on Lee et al.’s tag scheme: it does not appear in DBLP at all, no venue was found, and there is no code or data release. It is arXiv-only.

Lee et al. (arXiv:2212.08195) is the only system with an explicit symbolic control interface. BART conditioned on piece-square tokens, PGN, an attack list (White R_a1$P_a2), and five control tags supplied by Leela at inference.

Its weak point is upstream: the tag extractors score only 67–73% F1, so the signal linking board facts to text is itself noisy.

CCC (NAACL 2025) reads 20 concept vectors from LeelaChessZero T78 layer 40 via linear SVMs, trained on 200K Lichess positions labelled by Stockfish 8 eval terms.

Concepts are ranked by pre/post-move score delta and the top ones handed to GPT-4o. Its own human numbers are the field’s clearest diagnosis: fluency 0.91, correctness 0.60 across 5 raters (mean 1776 rapid).

Concept guidance buys style, not truth. Inter-rater Fleiss κ is weak away from correctness, 0.18–0.24. Code: github.com/ml-postech/concept-guided-chess-commentary.

C1 (arXiv:2603.20510) names the exact tension and resolves it with a prompt rather than a verifier. From §3.2.2: too faithful and the model parrots the moves; too exploratory and it hallucinates and loses grounding.

Reward and metric are both final-move exact match. The explanations are never evaluated.

Cui, Ling & Ng (arXiv:2607.11486) explicitly reject post-hoc justification and evaluate by Tree-Expanded Puzzle Playing. A separate player sees only the description and plays the puzzle against adversarially sampled replies.

Scoring is the resulting Stockfish evaluation. No LLM judge anywhere in the metric. Findings: main-line-only evaluation is insufficient, LLM judges are unreliable proxies for humans, and pure concept-based description yields little improvement.

One further result matters for anyone building a DSL: verbalising the strategy tree is lossy. The raw JSON outperforms every prose rendering of it.

4b. Commercial annotators

Chessprogramming.org was 503 throughout this survey, so these were resolved from primary sources — engine source, archived manuals, vendor help pages.

Product Verbal explanation? What it emits Source
Chessmaster 3000 (1991) Yes “Natural language advice in plain, clear English” 1991 manual (archive.org)
Chessmaster 4000 (1995) Yes Named command “Natural Language Advice” 1995 manual (archive.org)
ChessBase Fritz “Full analysis” Yes “variations, textual commentary… opening references” ChessBase help
Crafty annotate No PGN + engine lines + Informant symbols annotate.c source
ChessBase “Assisted Analysis” No Colour-coded board overlay ChessBase help
Shredder Chess Tutor No Arrows and symbols on the board Shredder site
HIARCS Chess Explorer No (apparently) Notation, symbols, statistics HIARCS site
Aquarium / IDeA Unresolved Output format not publicly documented —

Chessmaster shipped a natural-language move explainer in 1991, using the recipe everyone still uses. From the Chessmaster 3000 manual, Mentor → Advice → Detailed:

“Get more detailed advice and analysis, including the suggested move, the predicted following line of play, and the impact of the line of play on material points and position. The analysis is presented in plain English.“

That is engine PV plus material and positional deltas, rendered as prose, with user-set think time. Structurally identical to Automated Chess Tutor (2006) and CCC (2025).

By Chessmaster 4000 (1995) it is a first-class named command. No mechanism was ever published for either, so the lineage is invisible to the academic literature that reinvented it.

Crafty’s annotate is not a verbal annotator — a persistent misconception, settled here from source. It emits PGN plus engine variations and scores, gated by a margin threshold in pawns.

Output is .can, HTML with board bitmaps, or LaTeX. The complete set of English strings in annotate.c is the four status messages: which side is being annotated, for which player, and at what margin.

The only qualitative judgements are LaTeX macros from the chess package — \wbetter, \bupperhand, \wdecisive, \equal — that is, Informant symbols. Zero natural language.

This is why Sadikov et al. wrapped Crafty in 2006: Crafty itself does not do this.

DecodeChess is the architectural outlier. Its mechanism, in its own words: “Every suggested chess concept is verified to being relevant and important to the decoded position.”

An open file is asserted “only if it can be proved to affect the future course of the game,” using “vast searching abilities of the search engine (Stockfish in our case).”

Propose, then verify against search, then assert. Output is slot-filled templates and there is no LLM anywhere. It is the closest thing to a causal-faithfulness design that has ever shipped.

There is no paper and no patent. Google Patents returns zero for assignee Decodea and for inventors Ofer Shamai and Zeev Fine. Everything known comes from marketing copy that happens to be technically specific.

The product is dormant; the blog archive ends April 2023.

ChessBase “Assisted Analysis” is a colour overlay, not text: green for a very good move through red for a very bad one. Its stated design goal is to withhold evaluations so the user calculates independently.

4c. Symbolic, logical, and DSL

System Yr Formal representation Origin Available
Wilkins, PARADISE 1980 Production rules; conditions match patterns, actions post concepts into a database; concepts → plans → guided search Hand Papers only
Michie & Bratko, Advice Language AL0/AL1/AL3 1976–82 Advice = goal + constraints, compiled to a forcing tree Hand Papers
Bratko KRK strategy (re-formalised 2019) 1978→2019 7 named rules over room, critical square, rook exposed, rook divides, L-pattern Hand Open (LMCS)
Gadwal/Greer/McCalla, UMRAO 1990/93 Extension of Michie’s advice language → strategy graph Hand Paper
Automated Chess Tutor 2006 Eval-feature vector difference across the PV, plus an IF-THEN composition layer Hand+ Paper
ABML line (Možina/Guid/Sadikov/Bratko) 2007–13 Arguments attached to individual examples; ABCN2 unordered probabilistic rules Learned from expert arguments Papers
Guid et al., KBNK from tablebase 2009–13 ABML + specialised minimax → textbook instructions Semi-auto Papers
Caïssa AI 2025 Prolog predicates returning witness pieces Hand GPL-2.0

PARADISE (Wilkins, Artificial Intelligence 14(2):165–203, 1980) encodes knowledge as production rules whose “actions post concepts in the data base while the conditions match patterns in the chess position and data base.”

It discovers plans during static analysis and searches only to confirm the plan. Search trees run to tens and hundreds of nodes, with combinations to 19 ply.

The justification is therefore the concept chain that produced the plan, not a post-hoc score. This is the property later systems lost.

UNVERIFIED: the internal syntax of a PARADISE rule and its concept vocabulary. AI 14(2) and SRI Technical Note 509 are both unreachable online. Also unverified: the widely repeated claim that the test set was 100 positions from Reinfeld’s Win at Chess.

Advice Language. The exact AL clause syntax could not be confirmed — every primary text is paywalled or pre-digital. Do not assert the syntax.

What is freely readable is the resulting KRK strategy, re-formalised by Janičić, Marić & Maliković (Logical Methods in Computer Science 15(1:34), 2019).

Their auxiliary predicates are credited to Bratko: room (half-perimeter of the rectangle confining the black king, 15 if unconfined), critical square, rook exposed, rook divides, L-pattern, kings on same edge.

The strategy is seven prioritised rules — ImmediateMate, ReadyToMate, Squeeze, Approach, KeepRoom, RookHome, RookSafe — with coverage counts over all 175,168 white-to-move KRK positions.

That paper is the right citation for AL’s content, though not for its syntax.

Automated Chess Tutor (Sadikov, Možina, Guid, Krivec & Bratko, CG 2006, pp. 13–25) is CCC’s direct ancestor, twenty years early.

Crafty returns the principal variation; the system takes the vector difference between the eval-feature vector now and at the end of that PV. Positive components are goals achieved, negative ones are weaknesses created.

In their words: “The goals in our schema are simply the evaluation function’s features.” An expert-system IF-THEN layer composes basics into human-level features and suppresses redundant complements.

No evaluation is reported. CCC’s mechanism is structurally identical with learned probes substituted for hand-written eval terms.

Caïssa AI (Soliman & Ehab, KI 2025, LNCS 15956, pp. 148–160) exposes seven per-move predicates in reason.pl — fork_reason/5, absolute_pin_reason, relative_pin_reason, skewed_reason, and three more.

Each takes a (from, to) move and returns the witness pieces. A LangGraph module splits LLM commentary into atomic claims and checks each against the Prolog knowledge base.

Evaluation UNVERIFIED — paywalled, no open-access copy, no evaluation artifacts in the repo. Its reference [18] also cites arXiv:2306.12948 for ChessGPT, which is a number-theory paper; the real one is arXiv:2306.09200.

5. Motif vocabularies and the witness gap

The witness gap

Lichess’s shipped detectors are the de-facto motif DSL, and they are imperative Python booleans that discard their own evidence.

Every detector is a (puzzle) -> bool querying python-chess primitives: board.pin, board.attacks, board.attackers, board.checkers, SquareSet.between, plus helpers such as util.is_hanging and material_diff.

Two structural consequences follow.

They classify a puzzle line, not a move. fork returns True if any solver move forks. The output is a puzzle tag, never “this move is good because X.”

They discard the witness. fork() counts nb > 1 attacked pieces and throws away which ones. Contrast Caïssa’s fork_reason(Piece, Color, Pos, NextPos, ListOfOpponents).

Only zugzwang is engine-grounded, and it is a genuine counterfactual: push a null move, re-analyse at depth 30, and fire if win_chances(score) < win_chances(rev_score) - 0.3.

That null-move test is the only counterfactual construct in the entire shipped Lichess vocabulary.

The natural-language theme definitions live in lila/translation/source/puzzleTheme.xml and are maintained separately from the Python predicates, with no formal link. That is a soundness hazard for anyone treating the tags as ground truth.

Pattern languages that are not explanations

CQL (Costeff, ICGA Journal 27(4):217–225, 2004; CQL 6.2, free) is the real chess pattern DSL: declarative filters over PGN with move-sequence regexes and board transformations.

The transformations — rotations, reflections, shifts — are what make it a pattern language rather than position lookup. But it was built for finding thematic material, not explaining a move.

UNVERIFIED: whether CQL 6.x ships built-in pin/fork/skewer filters. The documentation page fetched does not list them.

Bizjak & Guid, ACG 2021, LNCS 13262, pp. 131–141, is the closest thing to a term-level motif DSL.

Static terms cover placement (Ra1), distance-decayed reachability (Rb1|0.89), connectivity — attacks B>pg7, defends R<Kh1, X-ray R=pa7 — and pawn structure.

Dynamic terms describe the solution line: ?px, ?ox, ?+, ?=, ?S for sacrifice, ?#, plus per-move markers. They are deliberately position-independent so motifs generalise across the board.

Retrieval is Lucene with BM25. On 400 expert-paired CT-ART puzzles, top-1 scores are static-only 0.252, dynamic-only 0.418, combined 0.481; top-10 are 0.433, 0.761, 0.814.

Dynamic beats static by a wide margin. That is the strongest published evidence that the reason for a move is not recoverable from the position alone.

An unused output interface

Chess already has a standardised, language-independent symbolic annotation vocabulary: Numeric Annotation Glyphs, from Edwards’ PGN specification (1994).

A NAG is $ plus an integer 0–255, with roughly 140 defined. Codes 1–9 annotate the move played ($1 for !, $2 for ?, $4 for ??); 10–135 describe the position; 136–139 cover time pressure.

No system in this literature targets NAGs as an output interface. A move explainer emitting $1/$21/$36 alongside prose would be gradeable against an existing standard and comparable across systems.

Engine grounding has regressed

Stockfish 15’s evaluate.cpp had a Trace namespace with named terms — material, imbalance, mobility, threat, passed, space, winnable — printed as an additive MG/EG table by colour.

That is gone. Master’s nnue_misc.cpp::trace prints only a bucket-level Material (PSQT) | Positional (Layers) | Total table. The per-square table its code comment still promises is no longer emitted.

Modern Stockfish has no term-level explanation at all. This is why both McGrath (2022) and Kim (2025) reach back to Stockfish 8 for a named concept vocabulary.

The Stockfish Evaluation Guide (hxim.github.io/Stockfish-Evaluation-Guide) preserves a JavaScript reimplementation, last updated August 2020.

6. Concept-based interpretability

Most of this work is global. It explains the model, not the move.

Work Unit Per-move? Causal validation
McGrath et al., PNAS 2022 sparse linear probe / CAV No — global None (explicitly declined)
Schut et al., PNAS 2025 sparse concept vector, L1-min under ranking constraints Obliquely (prototypes) No activation intervention
Pálsson & Björnsson, IJCAI 2023 probes on Stockfish NNUE No (states this outright) None
Karvonen, COLM 2024 linear probes on chess-GPT Yes (behavioural) Yes — 41% → 92% legal
Karvonen et al., NeurIPS 2024 SAE features No None — F1 vs ground truth
Jenner et al., NeurIPS 2024 residual activations, attention heads Yes Yes — patching + ablation
Lin et al., arXiv 2604.10158 transcoders + Lorsa on LC0 BT4 Yes — per-move pathways Yes — steering, zero-ablation
Chessformer, arXiv 2605.19091 cross-layer transcoder No None (authors flag it)

McGrath et al. §4.5 is the passage to quote. They argue intervention is the correct validation, then decline it, on two grounds.

First, chess concepts are mutually entangled — you cannot zero can_capture_queen without setting in_check. Second, internal edits risk pushing the network off-distribution.

So the field’s highest-profile “chess concepts in a network” result rests entirely on probe accuracy.

Jenner et al. is the methodological benchmark for causal work in chess. Patching the 3rd-move target square at layer 10 costs 1.88 ± 0.04 log-odds, against 0.55 ± 0.01 for the maximum over all 61 other squares.

Zero-ablating a single attention entry in L12H12 reduces log-odds by more than 1.5 in over 10% of puzzles — a larger effect than ablating all 4,095 other entries in that head combined.

Sandmann et al. (arXiv:2508.21380) is the sharpest negative result in the field, and nobody has built on it. Leela computes the correct solution mid-network, then overrides it at the output.

They call these “forgotten puzzles,” and steering against the learned prior recovers 61.7% of them. A probe fires and the behaviour does not follow. That is the empirical case against probe-accuracy-as-explanation.

Two reproducibility cliffs. AlphaZero’s weights and games are proprietary in both PNAS papers, so neither the probes nor the concept vectors can be re-derived.

Consequently every causal result in the field runs on Leela or chess-GPT, never on AlphaZero.

TCAV proper has never been run on chess. Both chess “CAV” papers borrow the vector construction and drop the directional-derivative sensitivity score. An unclaimed and cheap baseline.

7. Datasets

Dataset Size Explanation side Move-aligned License
GameKnot commentary (Jhamtani 2018) 11,578 games / 298,008 pairs free text + 6 category tags yes none stated
↳ mirror: ChessGPT annotated_pgn_free.tar.gz 12,770 GameKnot PGNs inline {} prose yes apache-2.0 declared; upstream grey
Waterhorse/chess_data (ChessGPT full) 37.6 GB / 461 files mixed partly apache-2.0 (mixed upstream)
Lichess puzzles 6,057,356 73 motif tags position-level CC0-1.0
Lichess standard games ~7.14B %eval, NAGs move-level eval CC0-1.0
Lichess eval DB 394,669,566 positions SF cp/mate + PVs position-level CC0-1.0
ChessBench (Ruoss 2024) 10M games / 15B annotations scalar win-prob — no language move-level CC-BY-4.0
MATE (Wang 2025) ~1M positions templated phrases move-level MIT
ChessQA 3,500 items (Semantic = 400) structured gold answers position-level MIT
Icannos/chess_studies 6,110 rows PGN prose + arrows yes CC0 self-declared
Shogi Commentary Corpus 4,327 games / 218,615 JP comments free text + factuality layer yes not stated

The GameKnot corpus is downloadable after all. Jhamtani’s repo ships only a Python-2.7 and PyQt4 crawler, expects you to re-scrape gameknot.com, and its license field is null.

ChessGPT’s release mirrors it: chessclip_data/annotated_pgn/annotated_pgn_free.tar.gz, 57.6 MB, 12,770 GameKnot PGNs with move-aligned {} prose. Rights are grey either way — forum comments were never licensed by their authors.

Correction worth recording: Zang/Yu/Wan 2019 released no commentary-dataset extension. They use Jhamtani’s data unchanged.

What they added is an engine-training corpus: FICS games with both players rated 2000+, giving 36M single-move records. They also drop the General category as “irrelevant to game analysis,” running on 5 of 6.

Lichess themes number 73. That is 75 categorized entries in PuzzleTheme.scala, minus mate duplicated across two groups, minus the mix UI pseudo-theme. One hidden theme, checkFirst, is never exported.

The generator is ornicar/lichess-puzzler, tagger/cook.py, AGPL-3.0. Frequency skew is roughly 2,500:1 — short at 50.5% and endgame at 50.0% against underPromotion at 0.02%.

23 themes are rule-derived staticThemes; the rest are vote-refined and carry crowd noise. cook.py still emits an overloading tag that appears zero times in the live export.

How much crowd noise a non-static theme actually carries is measurable, and for pin the answer is none we can find. Running cook.py’s own two pin predicates over the 151,614 puzzles of the probe set reproduces the exported tag on 99.76% of them, and the 370 disagreements sit 362-to-8 in the direction of cook.py firing where the export does not — the signature of the tagger moving on since the export was cut, not of votes overriding it. The pin tag is a program’s output, and the program is pin_prevents_attack or pin_prevents_escape (cook.py:115).

Label provenance is worse than it looks. Jhamtani’s category labels are SVM output on 297K of 298K rows — only 1K comments were hand-annotated, by two annotators.

The “Comparative” category falls back to a hand-written rule, the presence of the word “better.” Roughly 30% of comments are “General” with no chess content, and 23% are five words or fewer.

MATE’s “annotated by chess experts” means experts wrote about 20 template phrases per category, which were then applied by rule. It is not 1M hand-written explanations, and strategy classes are heavily imbalanced.

ChessBench contains no natural language at all. Its 15B “annotations” are Stockfish win-probabilities. It is an oracle, not an explanation corpus.

What does not exist:

No dataset pairs a position with a verified causal explanation.

No corpus of plausible-but-wrong chess explanations exists, so there are no negatives for training or evaluating a discriminator.

No human-adjudicated motif labels on arbitrary positions with reported inter-annotator agreement. Lichess themes are cook.py output on puzzles; Jhamtani’s are SVM output.

No cleanly redistributable expert-prose corpus. GameKnot is grey, ChessBase Mega and NIC are proprietary, and Lichess studies’ prose is user copyright even though the moves are CC0.

No move-aligned explanations at master strength under a permissive license, and no multimodal board-image-to-explanation pairing.

8. Evaluation and the faithfulness gap

Is there any benchmark measuring causal faithfulness of chess move explanations? No.

Every chess explanation artifact grades plausibility or chess-correctness: Jhamtani (BLEU plus validity judgements), Zang (BLEU/METEOR plus Likert), CCC’s GCC-Eval, ChessQA Semantic, SARFA.

GCC-Eval deserves a note. It is an LLM judge validated by correlation with human ratings, which under Jacovi & Goldberg’s guidelines makes it definitionally a plausibility metric, however much better than BLEU.

The word “faithful” appears in none of Jhamtani, Kim et al., or ChessQA.

Every causal intervention in chess targets internals, the board, or the training data — never a stated reason. No paper takes a natural-language chess explanation, intervenes on the factor it names, and checks whether the move changes.

Nearest things that exist

1. Pálsson & Björnsson, ECAI 2024, FAIA vol. 392, pp. 874–881, DOI 10.3233/FAIA240574. The closest precedent, and it builds a genuine causal ground truth.

Method: retrain the Stockfish NNUE with the concept’s positions skipped from training, via a patched make_skip_predicate in training_data_loader.cpp, so minibatch size and hyperparameters are unchanged.

Then play the ablated net against the unmodified one — 3,000 games each at depths 14, 15 and 16, so 9,000 per agent — and convert the strength difference to Elo.

Probing methods are scored by Pearson correlation against that Elo drop: 0.69–0.73 for raw probe accuracy, rising to 0.83–0.92 for the differential between probes on the regular and concept-removed nets.

The published abstract states that interpreting linear-probe accuracy as concept importance is “somewhat unreliable,” and proposes amnesic-style techniques instead.

It ablates training data rather than rules, and grades probes rather than stated reasons. But the gauntlet design is directly reusable.

2. Atrey, Clary & Jensen, ICLR 2020 (arXiv:1912.05743). The falsification design, in Atari.

From 90 papers they extract 46 claims across 11 papers that read saliency as evidence about agent behaviour. 87% reason backwards from the saliency pattern to a hypothesis, and only 3 of 46 carry additional direct experimental evidence.

Their key move is shifting the do-operator from pixels to game state. Pixel interventions “change the image in a way that is inconsistent with the generative process F”; latent-state interventions leave that process intact.

Three intervention classes — distortion, semantics-preserving, fat-hand — plus an invariance test: a static learned representation should give saliency invariant under semantics-preserving interventions. All three case studies come back negative.

Their verdict: “saliency maps cannot be trusted to reflect causal relationships between semantic concepts and agent behavior.”

3. Juozapaitis et al., IJCAI-19 XAI Workshop. The only verified ground-truth explanation evaluation in RL, in a gridworld “small enough to solve for the ground truth decomposed Q-values.”

It catches HRA producing values that do not reflect its own policy — a precedent for the claim that interpretable-by-construction is not automatically faithful.

4–5. Karvonen COLM 2024 (41% → 92% legal-move rate under a probe-directed residual edit) and Jenner et al. NeurIPS 2024 (1.88 vs 0.55 log-odds).

6. SARFA, Puri et al., ICLR 2020 (arXiv:1912.12191) — the chess explanation benchmark that exists, on the wrong axis.

Its Chess Saliency Dataset is 100 Lichess puzzles with pieces marked relevant by three experts rated over 2200, majority vote as label, methods scored by ROC-AUC. SARFA reaches 0.92.

The human study runs N=40 at ELO 1600–2000 over 15 puzzles: solve accuracy rises from 56.67% with no saliency to 72.41% with SARFA.

Huber et al. name the problem in print: this “does not measure the saliency maps’ fidelity to the agent’s reasoning, but whether the saliency maps coincide with human reasoning.”

Two results the field should cite more

Das & Chernova, IUI 2020 (arXiv:2002.04202), N=60, four conditions. The rationale generated faithfully from Stockfish’s utility function produced no significant gain.

Only the hand-augmented variant — adding criteria the utility function does not represent, such as capture-next-move and mate-in-three — beat the baseline. Faithful does not imply useful, demonstrated in chess.

It is also the only chess paper cited in Lai et al.’s survey of 100+ AI-assisted decision-making studies.

Shymanski et al. (arXiv:2511.03730) ran a placebo control in chess, with chess players. Satisfaction ratings could not distinguish an actionable explanation from a vacuous one (p=0.851) while performance diverged (p=0.0236).

Any chess explanation evaluation resting on human judgement inherits this failure.

The objection to pre-empt

Parcalabescu & Frank, ACL 2024: existing self-explanation faithfulness tests “do not investigate the correspondence between the LLM’s explanation and its internal processes.”

Instead they “design special LLM inputs and check whether the LLM returns self-consistent answers.” A movegen-level intervention changes what the engine can compute, which is materially stronger than an input edit.

That argument should be made in their vocabulary, not around it.

Two further framings are worth reusing. Jacovi & Goldberg’s graded-over-input-subspaces argument — “strictly faithful interpretation is a ‘unicorn’” — makes a restricted-scope faithfulness score principled rather than a compromise.

And Lyu, Apidianaki & Callison-Burch (Computational Linguistics 50(2):657–723, 2024) have a white-box, ground-truth-referenced evaluation category that is the thinnest in their survey precisely because ground truth is rare. That is the slot a rule-defined domain fills.

9. The recognition/computation dissociation

ChessQA (arXiv:2510.23948) scores 23 models across five categories. Semantic ranges 80% → 37% while Short Tactics ranges 77% → 0%.

Mid-tier models often sit at 65–75% on Semantic and 15–25% on Short Tactics. They recognise plausible-sounding commentary far better than they can compute the tactics that commentary asserts.

Lomasov et al. (arXiv:2510.26025) corroborate from the inside. Named human concepts decode at up to 85% in early layers but drop to 50–65% in the deep layers that drive performance.

A further 10–20% degrades on Chess960, once opening theory is removed. The CCC paradigm’s grounding substrate is weakest exactly where the model is strongest.

Fluent, plausible, and tactically wrong is the modal failure — and it is precisely what preference-based and LLM-judge evaluation cannot see.

Verified additions, absent from the current 64:

Deliberately excluded: chess2vec (arXiv:2011.01014) shares no task, dataset, or evaluation with this literature.

Also excluded as a generation system: SentiMATE (arXiv:1907.08321) runs sentiment analysis over commentary to learn a move-evaluation function. The direction is text → chess. Cite it only as evidence that commentary carries recoverable move-quality signal.

Already present and unchanged: McGrath, Schut, Karvonen COLM, Jenner, Sandmann, Lin, Ruoss, Chessformer, Maia-2, Kim/CCC, Tang/C1, ChessQA, Lee, Zang, Spinnato, Caïssa, Guid CSEDU 2013.

11. Open ground

Rule-level intervention is unoccupied. Nothing in this survey modifies the rules of the game to test an explanation.

The only counterfactuals anywhere are Jenner’s single-square piece corruptions, SHAP and SARFA piece removal, Lichess’s null-move zugzwang test, and Pálsson & Björnsson’s training-data ablation. Chess960 is used as a memorisation control, not an explanation probe.

No witness-returning, validated motif detector exists. Lichess returns bool; Caïssa returns witnesses but is unevaluated.

No compositional motif language exists. CQL queries, Bizjak & Guid tokenise, cook.py is imperative. Nobody has written a typed, compositional language whose expressions denote a move’s reason.

No concept-bottleneck model for chess — a confirmed negative. The nearest neighbours are RL-general: SCoBots (NeurIPS 2024) and LICORICE (arXiv:2407.15786).

Argumentation is applied to learning chess concepts, never to justifying a move. ABML uses arguments to constrain rule induction.

Epstein’s FORR (Cognitive Science 18(3):479–511, 1994), with multiple disagreeing Advisors collaborating per decision, is the only per-decision reasons architecture found — and it is game-general.

No ASP or Prolog chess-explanation work between AL3 (1982) and Caïssa (2025) could be found. A forty-year gap.

No simulatability measurement exists in chess. No study gives a human a chess explanation and measures their ability to predict the engine’s move against a no-explanation control.

Jhamtani’s Q2 — can the move be inferred from the commentary? — is the nearest, and it asks about the move, not the model.

Comment-to-game-tree grounding is unsolved in chess, per §3. It is upstream of the faithfulness question.

NAGs are an unused output target. An explainer emitting them would be gradeable against an existing standard vocabulary and comparable across systems.

Primary-source holes worth a library request

PARADISE’s production-rule syntax and concept vocabulary. AI 14(2) and SRI Technical Note 509 are both unreachable online.

The exact AL0/AL1 advice-clause syntax. Every primary text is paywalled or pre-digital; LMCS 2019 substitutes for AL’s content, not its syntax.

Michie’s “A theory of advice” (Machine Intelligence 8) versus “King and rook against king” (Advances in Computer Chess 1) — both cited as pp. 30–59, so one attribution is garbled.

Michie’s original 1976 Computer Bulletin AL0 note. No bibliographic record was found at all.

Možina et al.’s bad-bishop worked example. ailab.si/matej/bad_bishop.html now returns 404.

AI-generated

Deep-research summaries — xai-chess / ChessFaith corpus

One deep-research pass per paper (a sub-agent each): what it does, what it depends on, its results, its weaknesses, and its relation to the intervention-grounded chess-explanation benchmark. Generated from the verified 64-paper corpus; each agent re-fetched its source and cross-checked the project’s prior full-read notes.

Entries 1–64 are the original corpus. Entries 65–87 come from the move-explanation and dataset sweep of 2026-08-01 and entries 88–116 from the activation-oracle / symbolic-learning / circuits sweep of 2026-09-03; both are grouped separately at the end and their numbering matches PAPERS.md. Entries 65–87 cite authors by surname (see the citation convention note in PAPERS.md); entries 88–116 carry initials confirmed against the arXiv or publisher page.

  • Core (23)
  • Supporting (33)
  • Peripheral (8)

Core

Directly load-bearing: the methods, oracles, explanation engines, and faithfulness machinery the benchmark builds on or grades.

1. Acquisition of Chess Knowledge in AlphaZero

Citation: T. McGrath, A. Kapishnikov, N. Tomašev, A. Pearce, D. Hassabis, B. Kim, et al., “Acquisition of chess knowledge in AlphaZero,” arXiv:2111.09259, 2021.
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2111.09259 · Tier: CORE

Summary. McGrath et al. ask whether AlphaZero’s chess network internally encodes human chess concepts, and when/where they emerge during training. They train sparse L1-regularized linear probes (regression for continuous concepts, classification for binary) on the 16,384-dimensional per-layer activations to predict pre-defined human concepts (e.g., Stockfish evaluation terms, material, threats), producing “what-when-where” plots over training steps and network depth. They add non-negative matrix factorization (NMF) to discover move-selection factors not tied to predefined concepts, a behavioral comparison of AlphaZero’s opening-play evolution against human history, and qualitative commentary from GM Vladimir Kramnik. They find human-aligned knowledge is acquired, with many concepts emerging rapidly around 32,000 steps and material/eval probes reaching high R² (>0.75 after ~10 layers). Crucially, the authors state probing measures “only a proximate correlation… and not causation in any forms,” and that a probe “cannot tell if we are getting a confound or the concept itself.”

Depends on. Linear/concept probing and TCAV-style concept methods (Been Kim is a coauthor); Stockfish 8 evaluation terms as the concept vocabulary; AlphaZero self-play training; NMF for activation decomposition.

Results. Probes recover human concepts with high fidelity: Stockfish total-score R² exceeds 0.75 after ~10 layers at 64,000+ training steps; piece values converge toward conventional chess theory after ~128,000 steps; many concepts begin sharp accuracy gains around 32,000 steps (“rapid development” phase). Some concepts drop in later layers, suggesting information is discarded or re-encoded nonlinearly. NMF surfaces move-selection factors; behavioral analysis finds AlphaZero self-play shares similarities with but does not recapitulate human opening history. Explicitly disclaims causal interpretation.

Weaknesses. Self-acknowledged: probing is purely correlational (“not causation in any forms”)—high probe accuracy proves decodability, not that the network uses the concept for decisions. Concept vocabulary is anchored to Stockfish 8 terms and human priors, so undiscovered/non-human representations are missed by design. Confounding is unresolved: the paper itself flags that under an absolute pin, can_capture_queen cannot be intervened to 0 without forcing in_check=1 (plus other concepts), so probe signals can be entangled. Linear probes miss nonlinearly encoded information. Single-agent (AlphaZero) study; behavioral and Kramnik analyses are qualitative.

Relation to xai-chess. This is the canonical correlational concept-probing baseline that names the very pin / can_capture_queen-vs-in_check confound xai-chess resolves by causal intervention (board-level relocation and rule-level movegen patching), so it motivates the benchmark but must not be credited with causal validation.

2. Bridging the Human-AI Knowledge Gap: Concept Discovery and Transfer in AlphaZero
